Q2 2026 Report on Economic and Financial Developments | 1

---

<!-- PAGE 4 -->

Executive Summary 
–––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––– 
Global economic growth was uneven in Q2 2026 as geopolitical tensions disrupted energy 
markets  and  trade  flows.  The  Middle  East  conflict  drove  up  energy  and  fertilizer  prices, 
adding  to  inflationary  pressures.  Financial  market  volatility  also  increased  amid  shifting 
developments  in  US-Iran  relations.  Against  this  backdrop  of  persistent  inflation,  slowing 
growth,  and  heightened  uncertainty,  monetary  policy  paths  across  major  central  banks 
diverged, contributing to greater challenges for emerging market economies, including the 
Philippines.

These  global  developments  compounded  existing  domestic  headwinds.  Real  gross 
domestic product (GDP) grew by 2.3 percent year-on-year (y-o-y) in Q2 2026, slowing from 
both the previous quarter and the same period a year earlier. Weak investment and private 
consumption  reflected  elevated  costs  and  subdued  consumer  and  business  sentiment. 
Economic activity was further dampened by persistent governance concerns and spillovers 
from the Middle East conflict, including higher fuel prices and supply chain disruptions.

Inflationary pressures intensified. Headline inflation rose sharply to 6.8 percent in Q2 2026 
from 2.8 percent in Q1 2026, exceeding the National Government's (NG) 3.0 percent target 
for the year. The increase was driven mainly by higher food, transport, and utility costs amid 
the continued Middle East conflict.

In response, the BSP tightened its monetary policy stance. By the end of Q2 2026, the BSP 
has raised the key policy interest rate by a cumulative of 50 basis points (bps), bringing the 
Target  Reverse  Repurchase  (RRP)  rate  to  4.75  percent.  The  policy  adjustments  were 
intended  to  keep  inflation  expectations  anchored  and  limit  second-round  effects.  A 
measured  monetary  policy  response  will  also  complement  fiscal  measures  in  supporting 
consumption and strengthening business sentiment.

The  country’s  overall  external  position  improved  in  Q2  2026.    The  balance  of  payments 
registered a surplus, supported by strong financial account inflows amid higher foreign loan 
availments of domestic banks and reduced residents' loan claims on nonresidents. However, 
foreign  direct  investment  (FDI)  inflows  declined  amid  persistent  global  uncertainty.  The 
current  account  deficit  also  widened,  reflecting  higher  import  bills  for  energy-related 
products, capital goods, and manufactured products. Nonetheless, strong export demand, 
resilient remittances, and sustained receipts from business process outsourcing (BPO) and 
travel helped mitigate these pressures.

Meanwhile, the Philippine financial system remained sound. Banks' resources and deposits 
continued to grow, loan quality improved, and capital adequacy ratios (CARs) remained well 
above  regulatory  requirements.  Continued  reforms  in  banking,  capital  markets,  and 
payments, together with increased PhilPaSSplus activity, further supported financial system 
resilience.

On  balance,  the  Philippine  economy  remains  equipped  to  navigate  near-term  risks. 
Monetary  policy  will  continue  to  be  guided  by  the  inflation  outlook  and  incoming  data.  
Ample  international  reserves  and  a  well-capitalized  banking  system  provide  important 
safeguards  against  external  shocks.  Looking  ahead,  the  BSP  will  remain  focused  on 
safeguarding price and financial stability amid persistent external and domestic risks.

Q2 2026 Report on Economic and Financial Developments | 2

I.  Global Economic and Financial Conditions

––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––

Global growth was uneven in Q2 2026 amid disruptions to energy markets and trade flows 
due  to  the  Middle  East  conflict (Figure 1).  The  closure  of  the  Strait  of  Hormuz  pushed  up 
energy  prices,  raised  transport  and  import  costs,  and  fueled  economic  uncertainty. 
Consequently, growth forecasts were downgraded for most economies amid higher energy 
prices,  tighter  financial  conditions,  and  persistent  geopolitical  uncertainty.  Nonetheless, 
sustained investment in artificial intelligence (AI) and related technologies partly offset the 
broader slowdown in global activity.

Inflationary  pressures  intensified  across  economies,  driven  mainly  by  higher  energy  and 
fertilizer  prices  (Figure 2).  Rising  commodity  and  transport  costs  passed  through  to 
consumer  prices, reinforcing  expectations  of prolonged  restrictive monetary policy. While 
labor  market  conditions  remained  stable,  many  emerging  market  and  developing 
economies (EMDEs) continued to face limited job creation, high informality, declining real 
wages,  and  deteriorating  job  quality.  These  factors,  along  with  modest  income  growth, 
continued to weigh on productivity and household consumption.

<!-- PAGE 6 -->

Global financial markets remained volatile due to US–Iran tensions (Figure 3), causing sharp 
swings in oil prices and influencing inflation, monetary policy, and global growth. Volatility 
was high in foreign exchange (FX), bond, and equity markets earlier in the quarter, but risk 
sentiment  improved  and  equity  markets  recovered  as  tensions  eased.  However,  bond 
markets  remained  volatile  amid  energy  price  shifts,  inflation  expectations,  and  monetary 
policy signals.

Monetary  policy  diverged  across  major  economies  in  response  to  persistent  inflationary 
pressures,  weakening  economic  activity,  and  continued  geopolitical  uncertainty.  The 
divergence  contributed  to  uneven  global  financing  conditions  and  further  uncertainty 
across economies, particularly in EMDEs.

Q2 2026 Report on Economic and Financial Developments | 4

<!-- PAGE 7 -->

II.  Domestic Conditions

––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––

A.  Real Sector

The country’s economic growth slowed further in Q2 2026 amid soft domestic demand and 
lingering governance concerns. The recent Middle East conflict added to these headwinds 
through higher fuel prices and supply disruptions. The slowdown was particularly evident 
in weaker investment and private consumption, weighed down by elevated costs and still-
subdued consumer and business sentiment.

1.  Economic Growth

The Philippine economy grew by 2.3 percent in Q2 2026, slower than the 5.4-percent growth 
recorded  in  Q2  2025 (Figure 4).  This  reflected  the  contraction  in  investment  and  slower 
growth  in  household  consumption.  In  contrast  to  Q2  2025,  net  exports  contributed 
positively to GDP growth. On the production side, services and agriculture continued to drive 
growth, albeit at a more moderate pace. Meanwhile, industry output contracted.

The  county’s performance  during  the  quarter  was  also  among  the  weakest  in  the  region. 
GDP  growth  reached  8.4  percent  in  Vietnam,  6.0  percent  in  Malaysia,  5.9  percent  in 
Singapore, 5.3 percent in Indonesia, 4.3 percent in China, and 1.9 percent in Thailand.

Similarly,  gross  national  income  (GNI)  grew  more  moderately  by  2.2  percent  in  Q2  2026, 
reflecting  the  slowdown  in  net  primary  income  growth.  Net  primary  income  rose  by                        
1.0 percent, significantly lower than the 31.7-percent growth a year earlier, as compensation 
inflows grew by just 0.2 percent, compared with 27.7 percent in Q2 2025. The Middle East 
crisis and softer global economic conditions may have constrained compensation received 
by Filipino workers abroad, thereby limiting the contribution of net primary income to GNI 
growth.

On the expenditure side, investment remained subdued owing to weak public construction 
amid slower project implementation. Elevated construction and borrowing costs, alongside 
cautious business sentiment, constrained private construction and dampened spending on 
durable equipment.

In  contrast,  inventory  accumulation  rebounded.  Firms  replenished  stocks  to  support

Q2 2026 Report on Economic and Financial Developments | 5

---

<!-- PAGE 8 -->

production  needs  amid  supply  chain  uncertainties.1  This  may  also  reflect  precautionary 
stockpiling of petroleum products due to heightened tensions in the Middle East.

Household  consumption  moderated.  Non-essential  spending  weakened,2  particularly  on 
transport, restaurants and hotels, and recreation and culture (Figure 5; Table 1a). Essential 
spending3 also declined, primarily on food and non-alcoholic beverages. However, this was 
partly  offset  by  higher  expenditure  on  housing  and  utilities,  communication,  and  health 
services.4 Household spending was weighed down by elevated inflation, subdued consumer 
sentiment, softer labor market conditions, and slower growth in remittance receipts.5 The 
shift  towards  work-from-home  arrangements  may  have  also  contributed  to  higher 
expenditure on housing and utilities, and communication.6

Meanwhile,  government  spending  continued  to  support  economic  activity,  driven  by 
expenditures on public services and government operations. This included targeted social 
assistance programs implemented in response to the Middle East crisis.

Net  exports  contributed  positively  to  GDP  growth  as  exports  grew  faster  than  imports. 
Merchandise  exports  were  supported  by  robust  electronics  shipments,  particularly 
semiconductors, amid sustained global AI-related demand. Higher agricultural exports such 
as coconut oil also contributed to export growth. Services exports also rebounded, led by 
travel  and  business  services.  On  the  import  side,  imports  of  consumer  goods  moderated, 
while  imports  of  electronic  capital  goods  increased  significantly,  suggesting  continued 
investment in digital infrastructure.

On the production side, the industry sector contracted, reflecting weakness in construction, 
and mining and quarrying activities (Figure 6; Table 1). Construction declined further amid

slow  public  infrastructure  budget  execution,  elevated  construction  input  and  borrowing 
costs, and lingering governance-related uncertainty that weighed on investment sentiment. 
Manufacturing  activity  was  constrained  by  softer  domestic  demand,  persistent  cost 
pressures, and supply disruptions associated with the Middle East conflict.7

In  contrast,  electricity,  steam,  water,  and  waste  management  accelerated,  supported  by 
higher electricity demand amid warmer weather conditions during the quarter.

Meanwhile, the services sector expanded by 4.5 percent, albeit at a slower pace than a year 
earlier. The moderation reflected softer domestic demand and sector-specific headwinds. 
In  the  real  estate  sector,  elevated  construction  and  financing  costs  weighed  on  project 
development  and  property  demand.8  Financial  and  insurance  activities  likewise  softened 
amid a more cautious investment environment and tighter bank lending standards. Higher 
fuel costs and supply chain disruptions constrained transport activity. Meanwhile, weaker 
household consumption tempered growth in wholesale and retail trade.

In  contrast,  education,  as  well  as  human  health  and  social  work  activities  continued  to 
support services growth. This reflected sustained demand for essential services.

Agriculture  growth  slowed  to  2.7  percent,  reflecting  weaker  sugarcane,  palay,  and  corn 
production  amid  adverse  weather  conditions  and  elevated  production  costs.  These  were 
partly offset by stronger livestock, fishing and aquaculture,9 and mango production.

In June 2026, the Development Budget Coordination Committee (DBCC) revised its growth 
assumption for 2026 to 3.5 to 4.5 percent. With the economy posting an average growth of 
2.6  percent  in  the  first  half  of  2026,  achieving  the  lower  end  of  the  revised  target  would 
require stronger economic growth of at least 4.4 percent in the second half of the year.

2.  Labor Conditions

Labor market conditions continued to reflect the broader moderation in economic activity.10 
Despite an expansion in the labor force, the labor force participation rate edged down as 
the  working-age  population  increased  at  a  faster  pace  (Figure  7;  Table  2).  Overall

7 S&P Global Manufacturing Philippines PMI and PISM PMI as of Q2 2026.

<!-- PAGE 10 -->

employment rate declined, with the largest losses concentrated in agriculture, consistent 
with  the  slowdown  in  agricultural  production.  These  losses  were  partly  offset  by  modest 
employment gains in the industry and services sectors.

Employment  conditions  were  shaped  largely  by  developments  in  agriculture.  Adverse 
weather conditions and higher production costs affected agricultural and forestry activities. 
Wholesale and retail trade likewise reflected softer consumer demand. Meanwhile, tighter 
labor supply conditions, rising wage pressures, and workers’ increasing preference for more 
flexible  work  arrangements  may  have  also  influenced  hiring.  Employment  in  fishing  and 
aquaculture  remained  constrained  by  higher  operating  costs  associated  with  elevated 
diesel prices. Moreover, employment in other services reflected lower demand for workers 
in private households.

In contrast, labor demand remained firm in accommodation and food services, particularly 
restaurants and event catering, as well as in administrative and support services, notably call 
center and back-office operations. Employment opportunities also expanded in education, 
manufacturing, and mining and quarrying. The increase in employment in some industry 
subsectors,  despite  weaker  output  performance,  may  suggest  a  lag  between  hiring  and 
production or may reflect the continuation of ongoing projects even amid softer demand.

There  were  also  signs  of  improvement  in  employment  quality.  Employment  gains  were 
recorded  among  wage  and  salary  workers  in  private  establishments  and  government 
corporations; full-time employment; and medium- to high-skilled occupations. These gains 
partly offset losses among more vulnerable forms of employment, including self-employed 
workers  without  paid  employees  and  unpaid  family  workers,  as  well  as  wage  and  salary 
workers in private households and part-time workers.

By  occupation,  employment  losses  were  concentrated  in  elementary  occupations;  skilled 
agricultural, forestry, and fishery workers; and craft and related trades workers, which are 
generally lower- to middle-skilled occupations. Meanwhile, employment increased among 
professionals  and  managers,  pointing  to  continued  demand  for more formal  and  higher-
skilled employment.

Unemployment and underemployment rates increased in Q2 2026 compared to the same 
period last year. This underscores the need for stronger and more sustained job creation to 
absorb the expanding labor force and provide workers with more stable and higher-quality 
employment opportunities.

Q2 2026 Report on Economic and Financial Developments | 8

---

<!-- PAGE 11 -->

3.  Fiscal Spending

In Q2 2026, the NG fiscal deficit widened to 5.8 percent of GDP from 4.6 percent a year ago. 
This reflects weaker revenue generation amid subdued economic activity and challenging 
global economic conditions.

Revenue effort slowed to 16.7 percent of GDP from 18.1 percent a year earlier due to lower 
tax  and  non-tax  collections.  BIR  collections  moderated  as  slower  household  final 
consumption expenditure weakened the tax base for value-added taxes and excise duties. 
Customs  collections  improved  due  to  higher  import  volumes,  but  gains  were  partly 
tempered  by  the  temporary  suspension  of  excise  taxes  on  LPG  and  kerosene.  Non-tax 
collections fell sharply due to lower Bureau of the Treasury (BTr) income following the early 
remittance  of  dividends  by  government-owned  and  -controlled  corporations  (GOCCs) 
(Figure 8; Table 3).

At the same time, expenditure effort eased slightly to 22.5 percent of GDP from 22.7 percent, 
as  primary  spending  weakened.  Infrastructure  disbursements  remained  subdued  amid 
governance  reforms  following  the  flood  control  controversy.  Efforts  to  strengthen 
accountability and ensure the prudent use of public funds affected project implementation 
timelines and the pace of disbursements. This is particularly evident among infrastructure-
intensive agencies with slower utilization of Notices of Cash Allocation (NCAs).

Nonetheless, primary spending was supported by higher maintenance and other operating 
expenditures  associated  with  the  rollout  of  UPLIFT11  initiatives,  including  emergency  fuel 
procurement, fuel subsidies and discounts, and targeted cash transfers to vulnerable groups. 
Increased  transfers  to  local  government  units  (LGUs),  driven  by  higher  National  Tax 
Allotment  shares,  the  Annual  Block  Grant  to  the  Bangsamoro  Autonomous  Region  in 
Muslim  Mindanao  (BARMM),  and  releases  under  the  Local  Government  Support  Fund, 
likewise provided support to spending.

Interest  payments  also  reflected  higher  borrowing  requirements,  exchange  rate 
movements, and prevailing financing conditions.

Against this backdrop, the NG debt-to-GDP ratio increased to 66.0 percent as of end-June 
2026,  the  highest  level  since  end-2005.  The  increase  reflected  the  frontloading  of  both 
domestic and external borrowings to secure concessional financing, valuation effects arising

11  UPLIFT  (Unified  Package  for  Livelihoods,  Industry,  Food,  and  Transport)  is  a  whole-of-government  framework  established 
under  Executive  Order  No.  110  to  mitigate  the  economic  and  welfare  consequences  for  vulnerable  Filipino  households 
stemming from the ME conflict.

Q2 2026 Report on Economic and Financial Developments | 9

---

<!-- PAGE 12 -->

from  the  peso  depreciation,  and  net  availments  of  Official  Development  Assistance. 
Nevertheless, the country’s debt profile remained favorable. Domestic borrowings account 
for 67.3 percent of total obligations, while long-term instruments comprise 84.1 percent of 
the debt stock. This profile helps contain refinancing and exchange rate risks.

Meanwhile, debt sustainability conditions were supported by more favorable interest rate-
growth  differential  during  the  reference  quarter.  However,  this  improvement  was  largely 
due  to  inflation-driven  declines  in  real  interest  rates.  With  economic  growth  remaining 
subdued, debt dynamics remain vulnerable to adverse shocks. Moreover, persistent global 
headwinds,  compounded  by  heightened  geopolitical  tensions,  continue  to  weigh  on 
growth prospects while exerting upward pressure on financing costs. Domestic borrowing 
conditions have likewise tightened amid elevated uncertainty and risk aversion. At the same 
time,  recent  improvements  in  fiscal  indicators  have  been  supported  largely  by  transitory 
factors, including the early remittance of GOCC dividends and restrained public spending. 
This suggests that recent gains in fiscal and debt dynamics are not yet firmly entrenched.

Looking  ahead,  the  government 
is  expected  to  continue  pursuing  gradual  fiscal 
consolidation through stronger tax administration and revenue mobilization efforts, while 
maintaining  prudent  expenditure  management  and  prioritizing  growth-enhancing 
projects.  Sustaining  these  reforms  will  be  critical  to  rebuild  fiscal  buffers,  preserve  debt 
sustainability, and support medium-term economic growth.

B.   Monetary Sector

Monetary  developments  reflected  the  BSP's  measured  policy  tightening  in  response  to 
rising  inflationary  pressures.  Average  inflation  exceeded  the  target  during  the  quarter  as 
price  pressures  broadened.  This  prompted  the  BSP  to  raise  key  policy  rate  by  a  total  of                     
50 bps to anchor inflation expectations and contain second-round effects.

1.  Prices

Headline inflation increased markedly to 6.8 percent y-o-y in Q2 2026 from 2.8 percent in                
Q1  2026 (Figure 9; Table 4). This  is  above  the  target  of  3.0  percent  for  the  year.  Headline 
inflation accelerated due to higher food, transport, and utility prices.

Food inflation increased as domestic rice prices rose, reflecting the lean season as well as 
elevated costs of farming activities and logistics. Fish prices also went up owing to higher 
input  costs  from  rising  fuel  prices.  Transport  inflation  picked  up  following  successive 
increases  in  domestic  petroleum  prices.  Meanwhile,  electricity  rates  rose  due  to  higher 
generation and transmission charges.

Average  official  core  inflation  rose  from  3.0  percent  in  Q1  2026  to  4.1  percent  in  Q2  2026 
(Figure 10).

By region, headline inflation in the National Capital Region (NCR) increased (Figure 10; Table 
4a).  This  can  be  attributed  to  faster  price  increases  in  housing,  water,  electricity,  gas  and 
other fuels, transport, and food. Likewise, headline inflation in areas outside NCR (AONCR) 
increased  owing  to  faster  price  increases  of  food  and  non-alcoholic  beverages,  transport, 
and housing and utilities12 (Figure 10; Table 4b).

2.  Monetary Policy Developments

At its monetary policy meetings during the quarter,13 the BSP raised the key policy interest 
rate by 25 bps each, bringing the Target RRP rate to 4.75 percent. The corresponding interest 
rates  on  the  overnight  deposit  and  lending  facilities  were  adjusted  to  4.25  percent  and           
5.25 percent, respectively (Figure 11).

12 Refers to housing, water, electricity, gas and other fuels CPI component.  
13 Monetary policy meetings in Q2 2026 were held on 23 April 2026 and 18 June 2026.

Q2 2026 Report on Economic and Financial Developments | 11

---

<!-- PAGE 14 -->

The  BSP  assessed  that  inflationary  pressures  remained  strong,  as  elevated  global  oil  and 
fertilizer prices fed through to domestic fuel and food prices. At the same time, higher core 
inflation  pointed  to  a  broadening  of  underlying  price  pressures.  Moreover,  BSP  forecasts 
indicated  a higher inflation path. Average headline inflation was projected  to exceed the 
4.0-percent tolerance ceiling in 2026 and 2027 and to settle slightly above the 3.0-percent 
target in 2028.

inflation 
On  balance,  monetary  policy  tightening  was  warranted  to  help  anchor 
expectations  and  mitigate  second-round  effects.  The  measured  increases  would  also 
complement  fiscal  measures  in  supporting  steady  consumption  and  strengthening 
business sentiment.

The  BSP  will  continue  to  be  guided  by  incoming  data  and  remains  prepared  to  take 
necessary monetary policy action to ensure that inflation returns to the target.

3.  Monetary Operations

The  BSP  calibrates  its  monetary  operations  to  support  the  transmission  of  the  monetary 
policy  stance.  As  of  end-Q2  2026,  total  outstanding  placements  in  the  BSP’s  liquidity 
facilities stood at ₱1.3 trillion, broadly unchanged from end-Q1 2026 levels.

Liquidity absorption remained concentrated in overnight facilities, with a similar allocation 
mix from the previous quarter. The overnight RRP (ON RRP) facility and overnight deposit 
facility (ODF) accounted for 80.0 percent of total placements, while the term deposit facility 
(TDF) and BSP securities facility (BSP-SF) comprised the remaining 20.0 percent.

Average daily offer volumes for the ON RRP facility increased in Q2 2026. Meanwhile, average 
weekly offer volumes for TDF decreased  to around  ₱86.9 billion from  ₱96.7 a quarter ago. 
Average  weekly  offer  volumes  for  BSP  bills  also  declined  to  around  ₱51.5  billion  from                  
₱78.5 billion. The lower term facility offerings reflected the BSP’s liquidity forecasts, which 
considered the BTr's reintroduction of Cash Management Bills (CMBs) in June 2026.

Monetary policy transmission to BSP facilities remained broadly effective. The ON RRP rate 
remained closely aligned with target policy rate, while the pass-through of the cumulative

Q2 2026 Report on Economic and Financial Developments | 12

---

<!-- PAGE 15 -->

50-bp  policy  rate  hike  continued  to  improve  across  term  facilities.  Relative  to  pre-hike 
levels,14  the ON RRP  rate  was  higher by  45.2 bps,  equivalent to a 90.3-percent cumulative 
pass-through. The 7-day TDF rate rose by 41.8 bps (83.6-percent pass-through). The 28-day 
BSP bill rate also increased by 36.4 bps (72.8-percent pass-through), relatively more gradual 
than other tenors given the reduced offer volumes.

Meanwhile, money market rates widened in Q2 2026 amid persistent tensions in the Middle 
East. Relative to the previous quarter, the average interbank call loan (IBCL) rate increased 
by 9.2 bps, while the weighted average rate for interbank repurchase agreements rose by        
11.1 bps. Similarly, average short-term Bloomberg Evaluated Pricing Service (BVAL) rates and 
short-term interest rate swap (IRS) rates increased by 36.4 bps and 52.3 bps, respectively.

Longer-term  market  rates15  recorded  larger  increases,  reflecting  higher  term  premiums. 
Average BVAL rates outside of the short end increased by 88.2 bps, while average long-term 
interest rate swap levels rose by 92.7 bps. These movements suggest a bear steepening of 
both the government securities (GS) yield curve and IRS curve, reflecting risk aversion.

4.  Domestic Interest Rates

In the domestic primary market, Treasury bill (T-bill) rates increased in Q2 2026 (Figure 12; 
Table 5). Upward pressure on yields was amplified amid heightened inflation risks arising 
from  the  Middle  East  conflict.  As  expectations  of  a  tighter  monetary  policy  stance 
strengthened, investors demanded higher yields on GS.

In  the  secondary  market,  GS  yields  rose  at  the  short  end  of  the  curve  (3-month  to  5-year 
tenors), while longer-term yields slightly edged lower. The flattening of the yield curve as of 
end-June 2026 relative to end-March 2026 reflected elevated inflation risks and heightened 
macroeconomic uncertainty in the near term.

The BSP-US Fed policy rate differential widened to 100 bps in Q2 2026 as the BSP raised its 
target  RRP  rate  while  the  US  Federal  Reserve  maintained  its  target  range  (Figure 13). 
Similarly,  the  Philippine-US  interest  rate  differentials  widened  as  the  Republic  of  the 
Philippines (ROP) 91‑day T‑bill rate, net of tax, increased by 26 bps. Comparable US interest 
rates16 were broadly stable as the US Fed kept its policy rate steady during the quarter.

The  risk-adjusted  spread  widened  in  end-June  2026,  reflecting  a  25-bp  decline  in  the 
country risk premium17 and a 50-bp widening in the BSP Target RRP rate and US Fed Funds 
target  rate  differential  compared  to  the  previous  quarter.  The  country’s  risk  premium 
narrowed due to the combined effect of a 10-bp decline in the 10-year ROP yield and 15-bp 
increase  in  the  10-year  US  Treasury  yield  (Figure 14).  The  lower  10-year  ROP  yield  was 
consistent  with  favorable  investor  sentiment  toward  Philippine  sovereign  debt,  as 
evidenced  by  the  oversubscription  of  the  government’s  US  dollar  bond  issuance  in                        
June 2026.

C.  Financial Sector

The  financial  sector  remained  broadly  resilient  in  Q2  2026.  Banking  conditions  remained 
sound,  as  resources  and  deposits  expanded,  lending  continued  to  grow,  loan  quality 
improved,  and  capitalization  stayed  well  above  regulatory  requirements.  Meanwhile, 
domestic financial markets showed mixed performance.  The  peso  depreciated and bond 
market activity softened, while sovereign risk spreads narrowed and equities posted modest 
gains.  Continued  reforms  in  banking,  capital  markets,  and  payments,  alongside  higher 
PhilPaSSplus activity, further supported financial system resilience.

period.

1.  Banking System

The Philippine banking system (PBS) remained sound during the review quarter. Banking 
resources  and  deposits  continued  to  grow,  loan  quality  improved,  and  capital  adequacy 
ratios (CARs) remained well above domestic and international standards.

The banking system continued to consolidate in Q2 2026. The number of head offices stood 
at 460  offices in June 2026.  Meanwhile, the  overall operating  network, consisting of  both 
head offices and branches/agencies, increased to 13,688 offices from 13,633 in the previous 
quarter (Figure 15; Table 6).

The  expansion  in  total  peso  deposits  reflects  continued  confidence  in  the  Philippine 
banking system. Meanwhile, foreign-currency deposits (FCDs) owned by residents reached 
₱3.6 trillion, 9.2 percent higher than the level a year ago.19

On lending, the share of loans to production activities declined slightly to 73.8 percent in 
June 2026 from 75.8 percent a year earlier. Lending remained concentrated in real estate 
activities,  electricity,  gas,  steam,  and  air-conditioning  supply,  wholesale  and  retail  trade,  
manufacturing, and financial and insurance activities. In  contrast, the share of household 
consumption loans increased to 15.6 percent in June 2026 from 14.8 percent a year earlier, 
reflecting  strong  growth  in  credit  card,  salary-based  general-purpose,  and  other  types  of 
consumption loans.

Loan quality improved. The Philippine banking system’s gross non-performing loans (GNPL) 
ratio  remained  steady  at 3.3  percent  as of  end-June  2026  relative  to  the previous quarter 
(Figure 18; Table 8a). The net non-performing loans (NNPL) ratio, on the other hand, mildly 
declined to 1.4 percent20 from the 1.5 percent posted in Q1 2026. Compared to its regional 
counterparts, the Philippine banking system’s GNPL ratio was higher than those of Thailand, 
Indonesia, Malaysia, and South Korea (Figure 18).21

19 FCDs owned by residents, combined with M3, form part of a money supply measure called M4. M3 consists of savings deposits,

mentioned (LEM), substandard-secured loans, substandard-unsecured loans, doubtful accounts and loans considered as loss 
accounts.

Nonetheless,  banks  maintained  adequate  buffers.  The  NPL  coverage  ratio  stood  at                         
92.5 percent as of end-June 2026, higher than the 91.4 percent level posted in the previous 
quarter.

Banks also remained well capitalized.  As of June 2026,  U/KBs’ CARs stood at 15.0 percent 
and 15.4 percent on solo and consolidated bases, respectively, higher than the levels in the 
previous quarter and continued to be well above BSP’s regulatory threshold of 10.0 percent 
and international standard of 8.0 percent (Figure 19).22 Relative to neighboring economies, 
the  CAR  of  Philippine  U/KBs,  on  a  consolidated  basis,  was  lower  than  Indonesia’s                              
25.9  percent,  Thailand’s  20.3  percent,  Malaysia's  17.4  percent,  and  South  Korea’s

Bank  lending  continued  to  expand  in  Q2  2026,  albeit  at  a  slower  pace,  amid  cautious 
borrowing by firms and subdued consumer demand. Outstanding loans of U/KBs, net of RRP 
placements  with  the  BSP,  rose  by  9.8  percent  y-o-y  in  June,  down  from  the  10.7-percent          
y-o-y  growth  recorded  in  March.  This  expansion  was  driven  mainly  by  higher  lending  for 
both productive and consumer activities (Figure 20).

3.  Banking Policy Developments

Banking  sector  policy  reforms  in  Q2  2026  focused  on  strengthening  the  regulatory 
environment to support the soundness, resilience, and integrity of the financial system. Key 
measures included  the operationalization  of  the operationalization of the countercyclical 
capital  buffer  (CCyB),  enhancements  to  cybersecurity  supervision,  reforms  to  digital 
financial marketplaces and payment systems, and updates to investment, governance, and 
consumer lending regulations (Annex A).

24 The indicators used for money supply are: M1 (or narrow money), comprised of currency in circulation and demand deposits; 
M2,  composed  of  M1  plus  savings  and  time  deposits  (quasi-money);  M3,  consisting  of  M2  plus  deposit  substitutes;  and  M4, 
consisting of M3 plus FCD.   
25 Preliminary data as of 22 August 2026

Q2 2026 Report on Economic and Financial Developments | 18

4.  Financial Markets

Domestic financial markets remained broadly resilient in Q2 2026 despite persistent global 
uncertainty  arising  from  the  Middle  East  conflict.  Market  performance  was  shaped  by  a 
stronger  US dollar,  volatile  oil  prices,  and  shifts  in global  risk  sentiment,  alongside  higher 
domestic inflation and weaker growth prospects. These developments contributed to peso 
depreciation and softer bond market activity. Meanwhile improving investor risk appetite 
contributed to narrower sovereign risk spreads and modest gains in equities.

Stock Market

The Philippine equity market posted modest gains in Q2 2026 despite elevated inflation and 
persistent  external  uncertainty.  The  Philippine  Stock  Exchange  index  (PSEi)  increased  by          
1.5  percent  from  end-March  2026  to  close  at  6,037.2  index  points  (Figure 21; Table 10). 
However, the broader PSE All Shares Index26 declined by 0.4 percent to 3,319.3 index points. 
The  divergence  suggests  that  gains  were  concentrated  among  a  limited  group  of 
heavyweight companies.

Sectoral performance was mixed. The Services index led gains, while the Financials index 
edged up marginally. In contrast, the Mining and Oil index posted a steep decline amid the 
decrease  in  commodity  prices.  The  Industrial,  Holding  Firms,  and  Property  indices  also 
declined, reflecting elevated financing costs and cautious investor sentiment.

Domestic equity market indicators showed signs of improvement during the quarter. Total 
market capitalization rose by 5.9 percent to ₱19.4 trillion. Foreign investors, however, shifted 
to net selling of ₱23.3 billion from net buying of ₱7.9 billion in the previous quarter, reflecting 
continued preference for safer assets amid global uncertainty.

Market valuations remained relatively low compared with regional peers. The PSEi's price-
to-earnings (P/E) ratio stood at 9.2 times, well below those of Indonesia (13.6 times), Malaysia 
(14.3 times), Thailand (16.7 times), and Singapore (17.4 times).

Across  ASEAN,  equity  market  performance  was  mixed.  Thailand’s  Stock  Exchange  Index 
(SET)  and  Singapore’s  Straits  Times  Index  (STI)  posted  gains,  while  Indonesia’s  Jakarta 
Composite Index (JCI) and Malaysia’s FTSE Bursa Malaysia Kuala Lumpur Composite Index 
(FBM  KLCI)  declined.  Market  performance  diverged  across  economies  amid  differing

26 The PSE All Shares Index serves as a broader measure of the local stock market than the PSEi, as it tracks all listed common

domestic conditions and policy expectations. Still, geopolitical tensions and higher oil prices 
dampened overall sentiment.

Bond Market27

Philippine sovereign risk indicators improved in Q2 2026. The country’s 5-year credit default 
swap (CDS) spread narrowed sharply to 63 bps from 91 bps at end-March 2026. Similarly, the 
Emerging  Markets  Bond  Index  Global  (EMBIG)  Philippines  spread,  which  measures  the 
additional  yield  investors  demand  to  hold  Philippine  sovereign  debt  over  US  Treasuries, 
narrowed to 68 bps from 86 bps in the previous quarter (Figure 22).

Sovereign  spreads  narrowed  amid  improving  investor  sentiment,  supported  by  periodic 
signs of easing Middle East tensions and reduced concerns over energy supply disruptions. 
The resulting decline in global oil prices supported risk appetite toward emerging market 
assets, contributing to lower sovereign risk premiums.28

In the domestic primary market, local currency (LCY) bond issuances by both the public and 
private  sectors  registered  a  moderate  decline  in  Q2  2026  but  remained  supported  by 
refinancing  of  maturing  obligations  and  the  need  to  fund  the  NG’s  annual  borrowing 
program.29 Total LCY issuances amounted to ₱1.1 trillion, lower by 19.3 percent quarter-on-
quarter (q-o-q) (Figure 23). Government bonds accounted for 90.8 percent of LCY issuances 
while the corporate sector accounted for 9.2 percent.

Despite  lower  gross  issuance,  government  bond  market  depth  continued  to  improve. 
Outstanding  GS  increased  by  2.4  percent  q-o-q  to  ₱12.8  trillion  in  Q2  2026,  equivalent  to               
45.3 percent of GDP.30 Demand for GS remained firm, with auctions oversubscribed by an 
average of 1.8 times. T-bills and T-bonds received moderately stronger demand, with bid-to-
cover  ratios  close  to  2.0  times.  Resident  investors  continued  to  dominate  holdings  of 
onshore GS with 96.2 percent.

and a higher interest rate environment. Total trading of public and private debt securities at 
the  Philippine  Dealing  and  Exchange  Corporation  (PDEx)  fell  by  38.2  percent  q-o-q  to

continued to dominate secondary market activity (99.5 percent of total transactions), while 
corporate bonds made up only 0.5 percent (Figure 24).

On  a  real  trade-weighted  basis,  the  peso’s  external  competitiveness  improved  during  the 
quarter. The real effective exchange rate (REER)34 depreciated by 1.79 percent q-o-q against 
the currencies of all trading partners (TPI), 3.36 percent against advanced economies (TPI-
A), and 0.96 percent against developing economies (TPI-D). The decline in the peso’s REER 
was driven primarily by the nominal depreciation of the peso, which more than offset the 
effects of relatively higher domestic inflation. On a y-o-y basis, the REER likewise declined 
across  all  currency  baskets,  indicating  sustained  improvement  in  the  country’s  price 
competitiveness. The REER dropped by 3.98 percent against TPI, 2.64 percent against TPI-A, 
and 4.79 percent against TPI-D.

As of Q2 2026, the peso’s REER remained below its estimated long-run trend in Q2 2026 by 
2.0 percent (Figure 26; Table 11b).35 This suggests a modest degree of real undervaluation and 
indicates  that  recent  exchange  rate  adjustments  have  generally  supported  the  country’s 
external competitiveness.

5.  Capital Market Developments

Authorities continued to advance reforms aimed at deepening domestic capital markets in 
Q2 2026. Measures introduced by the Department of Finance (DOF), the BTr, the PSE, the 
SEC, and the BSP focused on broadening investor participation, enhancing market liquidity, 
improving  market 
issuance  processes. 
infrastructure,  and  streamlining  securities 
Collectively,  these  initiatives  seek  to  strengthen  the  efficiency  and  resilience  of  the 
Philippine capital markets while supporting long-term financing needs (Annex B).

6.  Payments and Settlements System

The  national  payments  and  settlements  system  continued  to  expand  in  Q2  2026.  This 
reflected  sustained  financial  activity  and  the  increasing  adoption  of  digital  payment 
channels, supported by the implementation of enabling regulatory measures.

In Q2 2026, the PhilPaSSplus processed 447,253 transactions, up by 7.6 percent y-o-y (Table B).  
Growth was driven by both large-value and retail payments, which rose by 6.8 percent and 
7.4  percent,  respectively.  This  reflects  the  sustained  intermediation  and  trade-settlement 
activity  among  financial  institutions  (FIs),  alongside  continued  shift  toward  electronic 
payments.

For large-value payments, settlement of transactions between FIs (15.4 percent), FX trading 
(9.1  percent),  and  government  securities  trading  (8.9  percent)  recorded  the  highest  y-o-y 
growth.  This  indicates  sustained  funding,  trading,  and  settlement  activity.  For  retail 
transactions,  PESONet  and  InstaPay  (7.8  percent)  transactions  and  customer  payments               
(7.6 percent) grew the fastest, reflecting the ongoing transition toward electronic person-to-
person and merchant payments.

36 The coefficient of variation is computed as the standard deviation of the daily closing exchange rate divided by the average 
exchange rates during the period.

Q2 2026 Report on Economic and Financial Developments | 23

---

<!-- PAGE 26 -->

The increase in the volume of transactions settled was accompanied by a 32.6 percent y-o-y 
expansion  in  the  total  value  of  transactions  settled.  Total  value  of  both  large  and  retail 
transactions increased y-o-y by 25.8 percent and 26.8 percent, respectively.

Large-value  and  retail  transactions  each  made  up  about  half  of  total  settlements  this 
quarter.  Customer  payments  (36.2  percent)  and  transactions  between  PhilPaSSplus 
participants (42.6 percent)37 accounted for the largest shares by volume. In terms of value, 
large-value transactions accounted for the majority of total transactions (80.4 percent).

The  expansion  of  payment  system  activity  coincided  with  ongoing  BSP  initiatives  to 
strengthen the safety, efficiency, and reliance of the digital payments ecosystem. During the 
quarter,  the  BSP  introduced  measures  to  enhance  consumer  protection  in  digital 
marketplaces,38    promote  fairer  and  more  transparent  fee  structure  for  person-to-person 
electronic  transfers,39  and  strengthen  incident  management  protocols  for  PhilPaSSplus.40 
These  reforms  are  expected  to  support  continued  digitalization  while  reinforcing 
confidence in the country’s payment and settlement infrastructure.

D.  External Sector

1.  Balance of Payments

The Philippines’ balance of payments (BOP) recorded a surplus equivalent to 1.1 percent of 
GDP in Q2 2026, a reversal from the deficit of 2.1 percent of GDP in the same quarter a year 
earlier  (Figure 28; Table 12).  The  improvement  in  the  overall  external  position  reflected 
stronger  financial account inflows, which more than offset the widening  current account 
deficit. The quarter's BOP outcome was shaped by higher external financing requirements 
arising  from  a  larger merchandise  trade  deficit,  alongside  an  increase  in financial  inflows 
that provided the funding needed to meet these requirements.

The current account deficit widened as stronger import growth outpaced gains in exports. 
The trade-in-goods deficit expanded by 22.0 percent to US$19.8 billion, reflecting increased 
import  requirements  for  capital  goods,  telecommunications  equipment,  manufacturing 
inputs, and electronic data processing equipment, as well as higher fuel import costs amid 
elevated  global  oil  prices.  These  developments  suggest  that  both  domestic  demand 
conditions  and  external  price  pressures  contributed  to  the  larger  trade  gap  during  the 
quarter.

While  imports  grew  faster,  exports  remained  supported  by  demand  for  electronics, 
machinery and transport equipment, gold, coconut oil, and mineral products. In particular, 
electronic  exports  continued  to  benefit  from  sustained  global  demand  for  AI-related 
applications and infrastructure. Export growth was likewise supported by demand from key 
markets, including the United States, China, and Singapore. Nonetheless, these gains were 
insufficient to offset the stronger increase in imports.

The wider merchandise trade deficit was partly tempered by continued foreign exchange 
earnings from services and income flows. Net services receipts remained broadly steady at 
US$2.1  billion,  supported  by  sustained  demand  for  BPO-related  services  and  stronger 
tourism activity. Meanwhile, primary and secondary income receipts continued to benefit 
from higher earnings on reserve assets and sustained remittance inflows, helping cushion 
the impact of the larger trade deficit on the current account.

Against  this  backdrop,  the  financing  side  of  the  external  accounts  strengthened 
considerably.  Financial  account  net  inflows  rose  to  US$9.6  billion,  driven  mainly  by  the 
reversal of the other investment account to net inflows. Higher foreign loan availments by 
domestic banks and reduced residents’ loan claims on nonresidents accounted for much of 
this  increase.  Meanwhile,  higher  nonresident  investments  in  government-issued  bonds 
provided  some  support  to  portfolio  flows,  although  foreign  direct  investment  inflows 
moderated amid continuing global trade and geopolitical uncertainties.

Overall, Q2 2026 BOP developments reflected the interaction of stronger import demand, 
higher  commodity  prices,  resilient  foreign  exchange  earnings  from  services  and

remittances,  and  increased  financial  inflows.  While  the  economy's  external  financing 
requirements  increased  during  the  quarter,  these  were  more  than  matched  by  financial 
account  inflows,  resulting  in  a  surplus  BOP  position  despite  persistent  uncertainty  in  the 
global economic and financial environment.

2.

International Reserves

The  country’s  GIR  fell  to  US$104.7  billion  as  of  end-June  2026  from  US$106.6  billion  end-
March 2026. At this level, the GIR is sufficient to cover 6.8 months’ worth of imports of goods 
and payments for services and primary income. It is also equivalent to about 3.7 times the 
country’s short-term external debt based on residual maturity (Figure 29; Table 13).

The decline in reserves was mainly driven by the lower value of BSP’s gold holdings, along 
with decreased FX holdings. These were partly offset by higher income from BSP’s foreign 
investments  abroad,  as  well  as  increases  in  the  BSP’s  Reserve  Position  in  the  Fund  and 
holdings of IMF Special Drawing Rights (SDRs).

As of end-June 2026, the bulk of the country’s total reserves, or 77.6 percent, were held  in 
foreign investments. Meanwhile, gold holdings account for 16.4 percent, while holdings of 
SDRs, FX, and the BSP’s Reserve Position in the IMF comprise the remaining 6.0 percent.41

Net international reserves (NIR), which represent the difference between the BSP’s GIR and 
total short-term liabilities, stood at US$104.7 billion as of end-June 2026, marking a decrease 
of US$1.9 billion from US$106.6 billion as of end-March 2026.

3.  External Debt

The Philippines’ outstanding external debt rose to US$154.9 billion as of end-June 2026, up 
by US$7.6 billion (or 5.1 percent) from US$147.4 billion as of end-March 2026  (Figure 30). The 
increase  was  predominantly  financing-driven,  reflecting  net  borrowings  of  US$8.2  billion 
during the quarter. These comprised reported net availments led by the NG, including its 
US$2.8 billion global bond issuance for general budget financing, as well as higher liabilities 
of banks to non-residents mainly to support asset growth.

The  increase  was  partly  offset  by  net  dispositions  of  Philippine  debt  securities  by  non-
residents  amounting  to  US$145.2  million  and  downward  foreign  exchange  valuation 
adjustments of US$459.0 million.

Public  sector  external  debt  rose by  US$2.9  billion  (or 3.0 percent)  to  US$98.5 billion  as  of 
end-June  2026.  However,  its  share  of  total  external  debt  declined  as  private  sector  debt

sector obligations, with outstanding debt of US$92.2 billion. Meanwhile, private sector debt

of private banks to non-residents.

Key external debt indicators remained at prudent levels, indicating the country’s sustained 
capacity to service foreign obligations in the MLT. GIR stood at US$104.7 billion as of end-
June 2026, which can service about 3.3 times the country’s STRM debt of US$31.6 billion. This 
indicates that external liquidity remained adequate despite the decline in reserves during 
the quarter.

The  debt  service  ratio  (DSR),  which  relates  principal  and  interest  payments  to  exports  of 
goods,  and  receipts  from  services  and  primary  income  (XGSI),  stood  at  9.0  percent  for 
January  to  June  2026,  indicating  a  manageable  debt  servicing  burden.  Meanwhile,  the 
external debt-to-GDP ratio increased to 31.6 percent as of end-June 2026 from 30.0 percent 
as of end-March 2026 (Table 15). The public sector external debt-to-GDP ratio likewise rose 
to 20.1 percent from at 19.5 percent over the same period.

Q2 2026 Report on Economic and Financial Developments | 28

---

<!-- PAGE 31 -->

III.  Conclusion

The Philippine economy faced continued headwinds in Q2 2026 from geopolitical tensions, 
volatile  global  financial  markets,  and  subdued  domestic  demand.  Inflationary  pressures 
remained  elevated.  In  response,  the  BSP  implemented  timely  and  measured  monetary 
policy actions consistent with its price stability mandate. Policy settings were calibrated to 
keep  inflation  expectations  anchored  and  prevent  second-round  effects  from  becoming 
entrenched. Monetary operations and financial sector policies complemented these actions 
by strengthening monetary policy transmission, supporting orderly market conditions, and 
preserving the resilience of the financial system.

Despite these challenges,  sound macroeconomic fundamentals and  resilient financial and 
external  sector  buffers  continue  to  support  domestic  stability.  Sustained  remittances  and 
receipts from BPO and travel helped cushion the impact of less favorable external conditions. 
Moreover, efforts to enhance revenue mobilization, accelerate the implementation of high-
impact  infrastructure  projects,  and  address  longstanding  investment  bottlenecks  will  be 
important in supporting economic recovery.

On balance, the Philippine economy remains equipped to navigate near-term risks. Monetary 
policy  will  continue  to  be  guided  by  the  inflation  outlook  and  incoming  data.  Ample 
international reserves and a well-capitalized banking system provide important safeguards 
against external shocks. Looking ahead, the BSP will remain focused on safeguarding price 
and financial stability amid persistent external and domestic risks.

Annexes 
Annex A. Banking Policy Developments

Cybersecurity  Maturity  Framework 
(CMF)  and  the  Cybersecurity  Controls  Self-
Assessment  (CCSA)  Requirement (BSP Circular 1232 dated 27 April 2026).  The  BSP 
amended  the  Information  Technology  Risk  Management  provisions  of  the  MORB  and 
MORNBFI by replacing the IT Rating System with the Supervisory Assessment Framework 
(SAFr)  and  introducing  the  Cybersecurity  Maturity  Framework  (CMF)  and  Cybersecurity 
Controls  Self-Assessment  (CCSA)  to  strengthen  information  and  cybersecurity  off-site 
surveillance and risk assessment activities.

The  Circular  requires  all  BSP-supervised  financial  institutions  (BSFIs)  to  conduct  periodic 
cybersecurity  self-assessments and  support  a more risk-based  evaluation of  cybersecurity 
preparedness.  The  framework  aims  to  enhance  cyber  resilience  and  improve  the  BSP's 
ability to identify and address emerging technology-related risks.

Amendments  to  Regulations  on  Credit  Risk  Transfer  Arrangements (BSP Circular 1233 
dated 18 May 2026). The BSP expanded the range of eligible credit protection providers by 
allowing  intrabank  guarantees  issued  between  a  bank's  head  office  and  its  branches,  or 
between branches located in different jurisdictions, to qualify as effective credit risk transfer 
arrangements. Previously, only eligible third-party guarantees could be used.

Overall,  the  amendments  provided  banks  with  greater  flexibility  in  managing  and 
transferring credit risk while ensuring that the use of intrabank guarantees remains subject 
to appropriate risk management and capital requirements.

Amendment of the Exposure Limits of Unit Investment Trust Funds (BSP Circular No. 1234 
dated 20 May 2026). The BSP amended the exposure limits of Unit Investment Trust Funds 
(UITFs) to provide greater flexibility in portfolio management while maintaining prudent risk 
controls.  The  Circular  revises  the  regulatory  limits  applicable  to  UITF  investments  and 
exposures, enhancing the ability of trust entities to diversify investments and align portfolio 
allocations with market developments and investor objectives.

The combined exposure of a UITF to any entity will generally remain capped at 15 percent of 
the  fund’s  market  value.  However,  UITFs  invested  partially  or  substantially  in  exchange-
traded  equity  securities may now have exposure of up to 20 percent to a  single entity  or 
issuer.  For equity index-tracking UITFs, the exposure limit shall be based on the benchmark 
weighting of the index component issuer, which may exceed 20 percent.

Framework for the Operationalization of the CCyB and Adoption of the Positive Neutral 
Rate (BSP Circular No. 1235 dated 20 May 2026). The BSP established the Framework for the 
Operationalization of the CCyB and adopted a Positive Neutral Rate as part of the BSP's Risk-
Based Capital Adequacy Framework. Under the Circular, universal and commercial banks, 
their  subsidiary  banks  and  quasi-banks,  as  well  as  digital  banks  (DBs),  are  required  to 
maintain a CCyB at a positive neutral rate set by the Monetary Board based on prevailing 
macro-financial conditions and systemic risk assessments.

The CCyB serves as a releasable capital buffer that can be built up during normal or strong 
credit  conditions  and  drawn  down  during  periods  of  financial  stress  to  support  the 
continued flow of credit to households and firms. The buffer may be adjusted as conditions 
warrant  but  shall  not  exceed  2.5  percent,  with  increases  taking  effect  12  months  after 
announcement.  Decreases  shall  take  effect  immediately  upon  such  announcement.    The

Q2 2026 Report on Economic and Financial Developments | 30

---

<!-- PAGE 33 -->

Circular  also  incorporates  implementing  guidelines  for  the  CCyB  into  the  Basel  III  capital 
framework.

Amendments  on  the  Authorization  Form  for  Querying  the  Bangko  Sentral  Records  for 
Screening Applicants and Confirming Election of Directors/Trustees and Appointment of 
Officers (BSP Circular No. 1236 dated 11 June 2026). The  BSP  amended  the  Authorization 
Form for Querying (AFQ) used by BSFIs in screening applicants and confirming the election 
or appointment of directors, trustees, and officers. The amendment requires the inclusion of 
additional identifying information, such as the complete maiden name of married female 
individuals, taxpayer identification number (TIN) or passport number, date of birth, sex, and 
last  known  address,  to  strengthen  the  BSP's  verification  process  against  its  records.  The 
amendments aim to ensure the qualifications of individuals to hold key positions in BSFIs.

Regulations  on  Digital  Financial  Marketplace  (BSP Circular 1237 dated 16 June 
2026).  The  BSP  established  a  regulatory  framework  for  digital  financial  marketplaces  to 
strengthen consumer protection, risk governance, and operational resilience.

Under Circular No. 1237, BSFIs that operate digital financial marketplaces must comply with 
standards  on  risk  governance,  data  governance,  consumer  protection,  and  customer  due 
diligence.  They  must  also  maintain  at  least  ₱1  billion  in  capital  to  support  continued 
in  technology  upgrades  and  cybersecurity  measures. Digital  financial 
investments 
marketplaces  provide  loans,  insurance,  investments,  and  other  financial  products  from 
multiple providers. They may also offer related non-financial products and services, such as 
travel, shopping, and automobiles, which complement bank or e-wallet offerings.

To promote consumer choice and competition, the Circular also requires the neutral and 
impartial presentation of financial products and services. It also requires the participation of 
at least three service providers that are not affiliated with the marketplace operator’s group 
or conglomerate.

The  Circular  likewise  requires  customer  consent  for  data  sharing  and  strengthens  the 
regulatory framework for digital financial marketplaces by enhancing consumer protection, 
mitigating operational risks, and complementing existing BSP regulations on outsourcing 
and cross-selling.

Amendments  to  the  National  Retail  Payment  System  Framework  and  the  Regulatory 
Framework  for  Merchant  Payment  Acceptance  Activities (BSP Circular No. 1238 dated                   
17 June 2026). The  BSP  introduced  measures  to  promote  fair  and  transparent  pricing  of 
digital payment services and  support  wider digital payment adoption. Under Circular No. 
1238,  fees  for  person-to-person  e-payments  across  banks,  e-wallet  operators,  and  other 
payment  service  providers  (PSPs)  should  not  be  materially  different  from  fees  within  the 
same institution. Transfers within one bank or e-wallet are often free, while any difference in 
pricing  should  mainly  reflect  fees  paid  to  the  network  switch  operator.  Micro  businesses 
such as sari-sari stores will also be allowed to open an account using the National ID or any 
official document. This may enable more micro businesses to accept digital payments and 
avail of other financial services.

Amendments  to  Regulations  on  Salary-Based  General  Purpose  Consumption 
Loans  (BSP Circular No. 1239 dated 18 June 2026). The  BSP  raised  the  maximum 
repayment period for salary-based general-purpose consumption loans to seven years. This 
applies  to  all  borrowers,  including  teachers  and  other  employees.  The  longer  repayment 
period  provides  borrowers  with  greater  flexibility  in  managing  loan  repayments  while 
continuing to promote responsible borrowing.

Q2 2026 Report on Economic and Financial Developments | 31

---

<!-- PAGE 34 -->

Under the circular, the seven-year period represents the maximum allowable tenor, and not 
a fixed loan term. Banks and other BSFIs must still determine the actual repayment terms 
based on the borrower’s capacity to pay. This reinforces responsible lending practices and 
supports the BSP’s mandate to promote financial stability.

For  longer-term  or  non-consumption  financing  needs,  borrowers  may  avail  of  housing, 
motor vehicle, or credit card loans. These are outside the scope of SBGPCLs and are therefore 
not  subject  to  the  seven-year  maximum  repayment  term,  even  if  repayments  are  made 
through salary deduction or similar arrangements.

Annex B. Capital Market Developments

Inclusion of Philippine Government Bonds in the J.P. Morgan's Bond Index.42 J.P. Morgan 
announced  the  inclusion  of  the  Philippine  peso-denominated  government  bonds  in  its 
Government Bond Index-Emerging Markets (GBI-EM) series effective 29 January 2027. The 
inclusion  reflects  continued  progress  in  capital  market  development  and  international 
recognition of the country’s sound macroeconomic fundamentals. It follows ongoing efforts 
to  improve  bond  market  liquidity,  develop  the  interest  rate  swap  and  repo  markets,  and 
streamline the implementation of existing tax treaties. The inclusion is expected to broaden 
the  investor  base,  support  market  liquidity,  and  strengthen  foreign  participation  in  the 
government securities market.

Proposed  Market-Making  Framework.43  The  SEC  released  draft  rules  establishing  a 
regulatory framework for market making in listed securities. The proposal seeks to enhance 
market liquidity and price discovery by formalizing market-making activities and aligning 
domestic practices with international standards.

Under  the  proposal,  only  SEC-licensed  trading  participants  of  an  exchange  may  act  as 
market  makers.  They  must  also  meet  minimum  capital  requirements,  demonstrate 
adequate trading experience, maintain a sound regulatory record, and have a valid market-
making  agreement  with  the  issuer,  the  relevant  exchange,  or  another  authorized  party. 
Market makers will also be required to provide firm two-way quotes during trading hours 
and  maintain  a  sufficient  inventory  of  securities.  Meanwhile,  the  exchange  will  oversee 
performance and regularly report compliance to the SEC. To encourage market makers to 
fulfill their obligations, the exchange may offer incentives such as reduced transaction fees, 
liquidity rebates, and access to enhanced trading facilities.

Reforms  to  the  Exchange-Traded  Fund  Market.44  The  PSE  proposed  amendments  to  its 
Exchange  Traded  Fund  (ETF)  rules  to  broaden  market  participation  and  to  facilitate  ETF 
listings  by  asset  managers.  Key  reforms  include  allowing  collective  investment  schemes, 
such as umbrella funds and unit investment trust funds (UITFs), to list multiple sub-funds 
lowering  capitalization 
under  a  single 
requirements for issuers; and providing greater flexibility in the appointment of authorized 
participants and market makers. The amendments also clarify the treatment of ETFs that 
track FX-listed securities.

Digitalization  of  Public  Offering  Applications.46  The  SEC  advanced  efforts  to  streamline 
capital  market  fundraising  by  requiring  the  electronic  submission  of  public  offering 
applications  and  reducing  the  processing  period  to  40  days.  The  SEC  released  its  draft 
guidelines  on  the  mandatory  use  of  the  Online  Application  for  Registration  Statements 
(OARS), a web-based platform that enables the electronic filing of registration statements 
and  related  documents.  The  platform  will  initially  support  applications  for  direct  public 
offerings, follow-on offerings, initial public offerings, and first-tranche shelf registrations. In 
addition, OARS will  also  facilitate  the  generation of International Securities  Identification 
Numbers  (ISINs),  Classification  of  Financial  Instruments  (CFIs),  and  Financial  Instrument 
Short Names (FISNs) in line with international standards.

1 Starting 1 January 2020, universal and commercial banks (U/KBs) are required to submit the amended reporting templates on banks' lending and deposit rates or Interest Rates on Loans and Deposits (IRLD) in accordance with Circular Nos. 1029 and 1037, 
series of 2019. Data are preliminary estimates as of 24 July 2026 given the ongoing refinements of the IRLD reporting system.  Bank submissions with interest rates of 100% and above were excluded per account code from the computation per the

Department of Supervisory Analytics (DSA) and Supervisory Policy and Research Department's (SPRD) definition of outliers.
2 Refers to the interest rate based on reporting U/KBs' interest expense and outstanding peso-denominated deposits
3 Refers to the interest rate based on reporting U/KBs' interest income and outstanding peso-denominated loans
4 End-of-period: On 3 June 2016, the Bangko Sentral ng Pilipinas (BSP) implemented the interest rate corridor (IRC) system to align short-term market rates with the BSP's policy interest rate, specifically the overnight reverse repurchase (RRP) rate. 
The interest rates for the standing liquidity facilities—the overnight lending facility (OLF) and the overnight deposit facility (ODF)—establish the corridor's upper and lower bounds, respectively. Additionally, the term deposit facility (TDF) serves as the

primary tool for absorbing excess liquidity.
5 End-of-period; On 8 September 2023, the BSP shifted to a variable-rate format with a pre-determined offer volume in the auction for the overnight RRP facility. In this regard, the BSP signals its monetary policy stance through the Target RRP rate.
6 End-of-period; On 18 September 2020, the BSP has offered BSP Securities as part of its initiative to shift to more market-based monetary operations. The inclusion of BSP Securities' issuance in the standard monetary operations of the BSP provides 
an additional instrument for managing liquidity in the financial system and supports the implementation of monetary policy under the IRC framework. 
7 Nominal interest rate less inflation rate with base year 2018.

1 Refers to the number of financial establishments which includes the head offices and branches; excluding the Bangko Sentral ng Pilipinas (BSP). Data include other banking offices which refer to any office or place of business in the Philippines other than the head office, branch or extension office, 
which primarily engages in banking activities other than the acceptance of deposits and/or servicing of withdrawals through tellers or other authorized personnel.  In 2017, per Circular No. 987 dated 28 December 2017, the term "other banking office/s" was replaced by “branch-lite unit/s”.

A branch shall refer to any permanent office or place of business in the Philippines other than the head office where a bank may perform activities and provide products and services that are within the scope of its authority and relevant licenses.  In this respect, a complete set of books and records

shall be maintained in each branch. A branch-lite unit shall refer to any permanent office or place of business of a bank, other than its head office or a branch. A branch-lite unit performs limited banking activities and records its transactions in the books of the head office or the branch to which it is annex
2 Include savings and mortgage bank, private development banks, stock savings and loan associations, and miocrofinance thrift banks
3 Inclusion of "Digital banks" as a distinct classification of banks was approved by the Monetary Board in its Resolution No. 1536 dated 26 November 2020. Based on BSP Circular No. 1105, digital banks are defined as banks that offer financial products and services that are processed end-to-end 
through a digital platform and/or electronic channels with no physical branch/sub-branch or branch-lite unit offering financial products and services. The Monetary Board, in its Resolution No. 693 dated 3 June 2021, approved the conversion of the banking license of the first digital bank,

Tonik Digital Bank, Inc. (Tonik Bank) from a rural bank.  It started its operations as a digital bank on 14 February 2022.
4 Include investment houses, financing companies, investment companies, securities dealers/brokers, pawnshops, lending investors, venture capital corporations, credit card companies, government non-bank financial institutions (i.e., Philippine Guarantee Corporation and Small Business Corporation), 
electronic money issuer (EMI) – others, authorized agent banks (AAB) forex corporations, remittance agents, credit granting entities (CGEs), trust corporations, non-stock savings and loan associations (NSSLAs), other non-bank with quasi-banking function, operators of payment system, and

virtual asset service providers
5 Include government insurance companies (i.e., Government Service Insurance System (GSIS) and Social Security System (SSS)) and private insurance companies  (i.e., life insurance companies, non-life insurance companies, and professional reinsurers) which only cover domestic and 
foreign companies (with more than 50% foreign participation)
*Based on the data as of 9 September 2026, released and posted on the BSP website by the Department of Supervisory Analytics (DSA) on 10 September 2026.

Technical Notes:
1. Balance of Payments statistics from 2005 onwards are based on the International Monetary Fund's Balance of Payments and International Investment Position Manual, 6 th edition (BPM6).
2. Financial account, including reserve assets, is calculated as the sum of net acquisitions of financial assets less net incurrence of liabilities.

3. Balances in the current and capital accounts are derived by deducting debit entries from credit entries.

4.  Balances in the financial account are derived by deducting net incurrence of liabilities from net acquisition of financial assets.

5. Negative values of net acquisition of financial assets indicate withdrawal/disposal of financial assets; negative values of net incurrence of liabilities indicate repayment of liabilities.

6. A negative growth rate in the net balance of the financial account and its components (i.e., direct portfolio and other investments) indicates higher net financial inflows or lower net outflows during the period relative to the year-ago level.

Conversely, a positive growth rate suggests lower net financial inflows or higher net outflows.

7. Overall BOP position is calculated as the change in the country's net international reserves (NIR), less non-economic transactions (revaluation and gold monetization/demonetization).

Alternatively, it can be derived by adding the current and capital account balances less financial account plus net unclassified items.

8. Net unclassified items serve as an offsetting account to the overstatement or understatement in either receipts or payments of the recorded BOP components vis-à-vis the overall BOP position.

9. Data on deposit-taking corporations, except the central bank, consist of transactions of commercial banks, thrift banks and offshore banking units (OBUs).

p  preliminary
. rounds off to zero

Q2 2026 Report on Economic and Financial Developments | 1

Executive Summary
––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––
Global economic growth was uneven in Q2 2026 as geopolitical tensions disrupted energy
markets and trade flows. The Middle East conflict drove up energy and fertilizer prices,
adding to inflationary pressures. Financial market volatility also increased amid shifting
developments in US-Iran relations. Against this backdrop of persistent inflation, slowing
growth, and heightened uncertainty, monetary policy paths across major central banks
diverged, contributing to greater challenges for emerging market economies, including the
Philippines.
These global developments compounded existing domestic headwinds. Real gross
domestic product (GDP) grew by 2.3 percent year-on-year (y-o-y) in Q2 2026, slowing from
both the previous quarter and the same period a year earlier. Weak investment and private
consumption reflected elevated costs and subdued consumer and business sentiment.
Economic activity was further dampened by persistent governance concerns and spillovers
from the Middle East conflict, including higher fuel prices and supply chain disruptions.
Inflationary pressures intensified. Headline inflation rose sharply to 6.8 percent in Q2 2026
from 2.8 percent in Q1 2026, exceeding the National Government's (NG) 3.0 percent target
for the year. The increase was driven mainly by higher food, transport, and utility costs amid
the continued Middle East conflict.
In response, the BSP tightened its monetary policy stance. By the end of Q2 2026, the BSP
has raised the key policy interest rate by a cumulative of 50 basis points (bps), bringing the
Target Reverse Repurchase (RRP) rate to 4.75 percent. The policy adjustments were
intended to keep inflation expectations anchored and limit second-round effects. A
measured monetary policy response will also complement fiscal measures in supporting
consumption and strengthening business sentiment.
The country’s overall external position improved in Q2 2026. The balance of payments
registered a surplus, supported by strong financial account inflows amid higher foreign loan
availments of domestic banks and reduced residents' loan claims on nonresidents. However,
foreign direct investment (FDI) inflows declined amid persistent global uncertainty. The
current account deficit also widened, reflecting higher import bills for energy-related
products, capital goods, and manufactured products. Nonetheless, strong export demand,
resilient remittances, and sustained receipts from business process outsourcing (BPO) and
travel helped mitigate these pressures.
Meanwhile, the Philippine financial system remained sound. Banks' resources and deposits
continued to grow, loan quality improved, and capital adequacy ratios (CARs) remained well
above regulatory requirements. Continued reforms in banking, capital markets, and
payments, together with increased PhilPaSSplus activity, further supported financial system
resilience.
On balance, the Philippine economy remains equipped to navigate near-term risks.
Monetary policy will continue to be guided by the inflation outlook and incoming data.
Ample international reserves and a well-capitalized banking system provide important
safeguards against external shocks. Looking ahead, the BSP will remain focused on
safeguarding price and financial stability amid persistent external and domestic risks.
Q2 2026 Report on Economic and Financial Developments | 2

I. Global Economic and Financial Conditions
––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––
Global growth was uneven in Q2 2026 amid disruptions to energy markets and trade flows
due to the Middle East conflict (Figure 1). The closure of the Strait of Hormuz pushed up
energy prices, raised transport and import costs, and fueled economic uncertainty.
Consequently, growth forecasts were downgraded for most economies amid higher energy
prices, tighter financial conditions, and persistent geopolitical uncertainty. Nonetheless,
sustained investment in artificial intelligence (AI) and related technologies partly offset the
broader slowdown in global activity.

Inflationary pressures intensified across economies, driven mainly by higher energy and
fertilizer prices (Figure 2). Rising commodity and transport costs passed through to
consumer prices, reinforcing expectations of prolonged restrictive monetary policy. While
labor market conditions remained stable, many emerging market and developing
economies (EMDEs) continued to face limited job creation, high informality, declining real
wages, and deteriorating job quality. These factors, along with modest income growth,
continued to weigh on productivity and household consumption.

Q2 2026 Report on Economic and Financial Developments | 3

Global financial markets remained volatile due to US–Iran tensions (Figure 3), causing sharp
swings in oil prices and influencing inflation, monetary policy, and global growth. Volatility
was high in foreign exchange (FX), bond, and equity markets earlier in the quarter, but risk
sentiment improved and equity markets recovered as tensions eased. However, bond
markets remained volatile amid energy price shifts, inflation expectations, and monetary
policy signals.

Monetary policy diverged across major economies in response to persistent inflationary
pressures, weakening economic activity, and continued geopolitical uncertainty. The
divergence contributed to uneven global financing conditions and further uncertainty
across economies, particularly in EMDEs.
Q2 2026 Report on Economic and Financial Developments | 4

II. Domestic Conditions
––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––––
A. Real Sector
The country’s economic growth slowed further in Q2 2026 amid soft domestic demand and
lingering governance concerns. The recent Middle East conflict added to these headwinds
through higher fuel prices and supply disruptions. The slowdown was particularly evident
in weaker investment and private consumption, weighed down by elevated costs and still-
subdued consumer and business sentiment.
1. Economic Growth
The Philippine economy grew by 2.3 percent in Q2 2026, slower than the 5.4-percent growth
recorded in Q2 2025 (Figure 4). This reflected the contraction in investment and slower
growth in household consumption. In contrast to Q2 2025, net exports contributed
positively to GDP growth. On the production side, services and agriculture continued to drive
growth, albeit at a more moderate pace. Meanwhile, industry output contracted.
The county’s performance during the quarter was also among the weakest in the region.
GDP growth reached 8.4 percent in Vietnam, 6.0 percent in Malaysia, 5.9 percent in
Singapore, 5.3 percent in Indonesia, 4.3 percent in China, and 1.9 percent in Thailand.

Similarly, gross national income (GNI) grew more moderately by 2.2 percent in Q2 2026,
reflecting the slowdown in net primary income growth. Net primary income rose by
1.0 percent, significantly lower than the 31.7-percent growth a year earlier, as compensation
inflows grew by just 0.2 percent, compared with 27.7 percent in Q2 2025. The Middle East
crisis and softer global economic conditions may have constrained compensation received
by Filipino workers abroad, thereby limiting the contribution of net primary income to GNI
growth.
On the expenditure side, investment remained subdued owing to weak public construction
amid slower project implementation. Elevated construction and borrowing costs, alongside
cautious business sentiment, constrained private construction and dampened spending on
durable equipment.
In contrast, inventory accumulation rebounded. Firms replenished stocks to support
Q2 2026 Report on Economic and Financial Developments | 5

production needs amid supply chain uncertainties.1 This may also reflect precautionary
stockpiling of petroleum products due to heightened tensions in the Middle East.
Household consumption moderated. Non-essential spending weakened,2 particularly on
transport, restaurants and hotels, and recreation and culture (Figure 5; Table 1a). Essential
spending3 also declined, primarily on food and non-alcoholic beverages. However, this was
partly offset by higher expenditure on housing and utilities, communication, and health
services.4 Household spending was weighed down by elevated inflation, subdued consumer
sentiment, softer labor market conditions, and slower growth in remittance receipts.5 The
shift towards work-from-home arrangements may have also contributed to higher
expenditure on housing and utilities, and communication.6

Meanwhile, government spending continued to support economic activity, driven by
expenditures on public services and government operations. This included targeted social
assistance programs implemented in response to the Middle East crisis.
Net exports contributed positively to GDP growth as exports grew faster than imports.
Merchandise exports were supported by robust electronics shipments, particularly
semiconductors, amid sustained global AI-related demand. Higher agricultural exports such
as coconut oil also contributed to export growth. Services exports also rebounded, led by
travel and business services. On the import side, imports of consumer goods moderated,
while imports of electronic capital goods increased significantly, suggesting continued
investment in digital infrastructure.
On the production side, the industry sector contracted, reflecting weakness in construction,
and mining and quarrying activities (Figure 6; Table 1). Construction declined further amid
